Keywords: tutorial / skill / recipe / audit / governance / cedar / receipts / ed25519.

3. **`review-agent-governance`** v0.1.0 — Description: "Require a human approval signal before an AI agent can post PR reviews, comments, merges, or writes to CI configuration. Joins protect-mcp and signed-audit-trails in the governance category; composes with protect-mcp for runtime enforcement." Same author + homepage. Keywords: review / governance / cedar / receipts / human-approval / pr-review / ci-guard.

Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:128:to `exit 0` without calling `protect-mcp evaluate`. The downstream
Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:129:`PostToolUse` receipt for that approved action will therefore have

Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:131:was evaluated. Auditors walking the chain should expect this: an approved
Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:132:tool call shows up as a signed receipt with `reason: human_approved` and
Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:133:no policy reference. Denied tool calls and non-review actions (which do
Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:134:go through Cedar) carry the `policy_digest` as usual.

Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:190:that proves every receipt is authentic and the chain is intact. If any
Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:191:receipt was altered after signing, verification fails with exit 1.
Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance\README.md:195:- **Ed25519** (RFC 8032) for receipt signatures

6:# protect-mcp — Policy Enforcement + Signed Receipts
7:
8:Cryptographic governance for every Claude Code tool call. Each invocation is
9:evaluated against a Cedar policy and produces an Ed25519-signed receipt that
10:anyone can verify offline.
11:
12:## Overview
13:
14:Claude Code runs powerful tools: `Bash`, `Edit`, `Write`, `WebFetch`. By default
15:there is no audit trail, no policy enforcement, and no way to prove what was
16:decided after the fact. `protect-mcp` closes all three gaps:
17:
18:- **Cedar policies** (AWS's open authorization engine) evaluate every tool call
19:  before execution. Cedar deny is authoritative.
20:- **Ed25519 receipts** record each decision with its inputs, the policy that
21:  governed it, and the outcome. Receipts are hash-chained.
22:- **Offline verification** via `npx @veritasacta/verify`. No server, no account,
23:  no trust in the operator.
24:
25:## Problem
26:
27:AI agents make decisions that affect money, safety, and rights. The Claude Code
28:session log records what happened, but the log is:
29:
30:- Mutable — anyone with access can edit it
31:- Unsigned — there is no way to prove integrity
32:- Operator-bound — verification requires trusting whoever holds the log
33:
34:For compliance contexts (finance, healthcare, regulated research), this is not
35:sufficient. You need tamper-evident evidence that can be verified by third
36:parties without trusting you.
37:
38:## Solution
39:
40:Add `protect-mcp` to your Claude Code project:
41:
42:```bash
43:# 1. Install the plugin (adds hooks + skill to your project)
44:claude plugin install wshobson/agents/protect-mcp
45:
46:# 2. Configure hooks in .claude/settings.json (see below)
47:
48:# 3. Start the receipt-signing server (runs locally, no external calls)
49:npx protect-mcp@latest serve --enforce
50:
51:# 4. Use Claude Code normally. Every tool call is now policy-evaluated
52:#    and produces a signed receipt in ./receipts/
53:```
54:
55:## Hook Configuration
56:
57:Add the following to your project's `.claude/settings.json`:
58:
59:```json
60:{
61:  "hooks": {
62:    "PreToolUse": [
63:      {
64:        "matcher": ".*",
65:        "hook": {
66:          "type": "command",
67:          "command": "npx protect-mcp@latest evaluate --policy ./protect.cedar --tool \"$TOOL_NAME\" --input \"$TOOL_INPUT\" || exit 2"
68:        }
69:      }
70:    ],
71:    "PostToolUse": [
72:      {
73:        "matcher": ".*",
74:        "hook": {
75:          "type": "command",
76:          "command": "npx protect-mcp@latest sign --tool \"$TOOL_NAME\" --input \"$TOOL_INPUT\" --output \"$TOOL_OUTPUT\" --receipts ./receipts/"
77:        }
78:      }
79:    ]
80:  }
81:}
82:```
83:
84:### What each hook does
85:
86:**PreToolUse** — Runs BEFORE the tool executes. Evaluates the tool call against
87:your Cedar policy file. If Cedar returns `deny`, the hook exits with code 2 and
88:Claude Code blocks the tool call entirely.
89:
90:**PostToolUse** — Runs AFTER the tool completes. Signs a receipt containing the
91:tool name, input hash, output hash, decision, policy digest, and timestamp.
92:Writes the receipt to `./receipts/<timestamp>.json`.
93:
94:## Cedar Policy File
95:
96:Create `./protect.cedar` at the project root:
97:
98:```cedar
99:// Allow read-only tools by default
100:permit (
101:    principal,
102:    action in [Action::"Read", Action::"Glob", Action::"Grep", Action::"WebFetch"],
103:    resource
104:);
105:
106:// Require explicit allow for destructive tools
107:permit (
108:    principal,
109:    action == Action::"Bash",
110:    resource
111:) when {
112:    // Allow safe commands only
113:    context.command_pattern in ["git", "npm", "ls", "cat", "echo", "pwd", "test"]
114:};
115:
116:// Never allow recursive deletion
117:forbid (
118:    principal,
119:    action == Action::"Bash",
120:    resource
121:) when {
122:    context.command_pattern == "rm -rf"
123:};
124:
125:// Require confirmation for writes outside the project
126:forbid (
127:    principal,
128:    action in [Action::"Edit", Action::"Write"],
129:    resource
130:) when {
131:    context.path_starts_with != "."
132:};
133:```
134:
135:## Verification
136:
137:Verify a single receipt:
138:
139:```bash
140:npx @veritasacta/verify receipts/2026-04-15T10-30-00Z.json
141:# Exit 0 = valid
142:# Exit 1 = tampered
143:# Exit 2 = malformed
144:```
145:
146:Verify the entire chain:
147:
148:```bash
149:npx @veritasacta/verify receipts/*.json
150:```
151:
152:Use the plugin's slash commands from within Claude Code:
153:
154:```
155:/verify-receipt receipts/latest.json
156:/audit-chain ./receipts/ --last 20
157:```
158:
159:## Receipt Format
160:
161:Each receipt is a JSON file with this structure:
162:
163:```json
164:{
165:  "receipt_id": "rec_8f92a3b1",
166:  "receipt_version": "1.0",
167:  "issuer_id": "claude-code-protect-mcp",
168:  "event_time": "2026-04-15T10:30:00.000Z",
169:  "tool_name": "Bash",
170:  "input_hash": "sha256:a3f8...",
171:  "decision": "allow",
172:  "policy_id": "autoresearch-safe",
173:  "policy_digest": "sha256:b7e2...",
174:  "parent_receipt_id": "rec_3d1ab7c2",
175:  "public_key": "4437ca56815c0516...",
176:  "signature": "4cde814b7889e987..."
177:}
178:```
179:
180:- **Ed25519** signatures (RFC 8032)
181:- **JCS canonicalization** (RFC 8785) before signing
182:- **Hash-chained** to the previous receipt via `parent_receipt_id`
183:- **Offline verifiable** — no network call, no vendor lookup
184:
185:## Why This Matters
186:
187:| Before | After |
188:|--------|-------|
189:| "Trust me, the agent only read files" | Cryptographically provable: every Read logged and signed |
190:| "The log shows it happened" | The receipt proves it happened, and no one can edit it |
191:| "You'd have to audit our system" | Anyone can verify every receipt offline |
192:| "Logs might be different by now" | Ed25519 signatures lock the record at signing time |
193:
194:## Standards
195:
196:- **Ed25519** — RFC 8032 (digital signatures)
197:- **JCS** — RFC 8785 (deterministic JSON canonicalization)
198:- **Cedar** — AWS's open authorization policy language
199:- **IETF draft** — [draft-farley-acta-signed-receipts](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-farley-acta-signed-receipts/)
200:
201:## Related
202:
203:- **npm**: [protect-mcp](https://www.npmjs.com/package/protect-mcp) (v0.5.5, 10K+ monthly downloads)
204:- **Verify CLI**: [@veritasacta/verify](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@veritasacta/verify)
205:- **Source**: [github.com/ScopeBlind/scopeblind-gateway](https://github.com/ScopeBlind/scopeblind-gateway)
206:- **Protocol**: [veritasacta.com](https://veritasacta.com)
207:- **Integrations**: Microsoft Agent Governance Toolkit (PR #667), AWS cedar-policy/cedar-for-agents (PR #64)
FILE Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/protect-mcp/test/README.md
1:# protect-mcp test fixtures
2:
3:Round-trip tests for the `protect-mcp` plugin's `PreToolUse` and `PostToolUse`
4:hooks. Exercises the full evaluate → sign → verify loop against deterministic
5:fixtures, including the tamper-detection path.
6:
7:## Layout
8:
9:```
10:test/
11:├── fixtures/
12:│   ├── test-policy.cedar                  # Cedar policy used by all tests
13:│   ├── pretool-allow-read.json            # Read should be permitted
14:│   ├── pretool-allow-bash-safe.json       # Bash "git status" should be permitted
15:│   ├── pretool-deny-bash-destructive.json # Bash "rm -rf /" should be denied
16:│   ├── pretool-deny-write.json            # Write should be denied
17:│   └── posttool-signing-input.json        # Input for receipt signing
18:├── expected/
19:│   └── receipt-schema.json                # Expected receipt shape (JSON Schema)
20:├── run-tests.sh                           # Full round-trip (requires node / npx)
21:└── verify-fixtures.sh                     # Static validation (python3 only)
22:```
23:
24:## Running
25:
26:### Full round-trip (local development)
27:
28:```bash
29:./run-tests.sh
30:```
31:
32:Requires `node` (>= 18), `npx`, and `python3`. Fetches `protect-mcp` and
33:`@veritasacta/verify` from npm on first run. Runs eight tests:
34:
35:| # | Scenario | Expected exit |
36:|---|----------|----------------|
37:| 1 | `PreToolUse` on `Read`                         | 0 (permit)  |
38:| 2 | `PreToolUse` on `Bash git status`              | 0 (permit)  |
39:| 3 | `PreToolUse` on `Bash rm -rf /`                | 2 (forbid)  |
40:| 4 | `PreToolUse` on `Write`                        | 2 (forbid)  |
41:| 5 | `PostToolUse` signing produces a receipt file  | 0 (success) |
42:| 6 | Produced receipt conforms to the schema        | 0 (valid)   |
43:| 7 | `@veritasacta/verify` accepts the receipt      | 0 (valid)   |
44:| 8 | Tampered receipt is rejected                   | 1 (tampered)|
45:
46:Test 8 is the critical regression guard: flipping the `decision` field in a
47:signed receipt must invalidate the Ed25519 signature, so `@veritasacta/verify`
48:must exit 1 rather than 0.
49:
50:### Static validation (CI-safe)
51:
52:```bash
53:./verify-fixtures.sh
54:```
55:
56:Only requires `python3`. Validates that every fixture is well-formed JSON and
57:has the expected structure. No network calls, no npm fetches. Safe to run in
58:sandboxed or offline CI.
59:
60:## What the tests prove
61:
62:- **Policy evaluation:** Cedar `permit` and `forbid` rules produce the
63:  expected exit codes (0 / 2).
64:- **Receipt schema:** signed receipts include every required field from
65:  [`draft-farley-acta-signed-receipts`](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-farley-acta-signed-receipts/).
66:- **Signature integrity:** `@veritasacta/verify` validates authentic
67:  receipts and rejects tampered ones, with the documented exit codes.
68:- **End-to-end integration:** the plugin's two hooks compose into a
69:  working allow/deny + sign + verify pipeline.
70:
71:## Extending
72:
73:To add a new test case:
74:
75:1. Drop a `pretool-*.json` or `posttool-*.json` fixture into `fixtures/`
76:2. Add a matching rule to `fixtures/test-policy.cedar` if the test needs one
77:3. Add an assertion block to `run-tests.sh` mirroring the existing ones
78:
79:Follow the naming convention `pretool-<allow|deny>-<scenario>.json` so the
80:intent is obvious from `ls fixtures/`.
81:
82:## Exit codes
83:
84:| Script             | Exit | Meaning |
85:|--------------------|------|---------|
86:| `run-tests.sh`     | 0    | All tests passed |
87:| `run-tests.sh`     | 1    | One or more tests failed |
88:| `run-tests.sh`     | 77   | Required tool missing (skipped in CI) |
89:| `verify-fixtures.sh` | 0  | All fixtures valid |
90:| `verify-fixtures.sh` | 1  | Fixture malformed |
91:| `verify-fixtures.sh` | 77 | `python3` missing (skipped) |
92:
93:77 is the autotools convention for "skip this test" and is interpreted as a
94:skip by most CI frameworks.

1:# signed-audit-trails
2:
3:A teaching skill for setting up cryptographically signed audit trails on every
4:Claude Code tool call. Cookbook-style walkthrough with runnable examples.
5:
6:## What this is
7:
8:A **skill** (not a runtime hook): a set of instructions and examples that
9:explain the pattern end-to-end. Use this when you are figuring out whether
10:receipts are the right fit for your project. Once you know they are, install
11:the [`protect-mcp`](../protect-mcp/) plugin for the actual hooks.
12:
13:## When to use this plugin
14:
15:- **Learning** the pattern before committing to infrastructure
16:- **Evaluating** whether signed audit trails fit your compliance need
17:- **Teaching** team members the three-invariant cryptographic model
18:  (JCS canonicalization + Ed25519 signatures + hash chains)
19:- **Walking a client or auditor** through a live demonstration of tamper
20:  detection
21:
22:For production use, the [`protect-mcp`](../protect-mcp/) plugin gives you the
23:runtime hooks directly. This plugin is the skill file you invoke via
24:`Skill` when you want the concept explained in-session.
25:

28:```
29:skills/signed-audit-trails-recipe/SKILL.md

32:A single skill file containing:

35:- Live tamper detection walkthrough

37:- Cross-implementation interoperability table
38:- CI/CD integration snippet (GitHub Actions)
39:- Composition with SLSA provenance for agent-built software
40:- Common pitfalls and references
41:
42:## Standards
43:
44:- **Ed25519** (RFC 8032) for receipt signatures
45:- **JCS** (RFC 8785) for deterministic JSON canonicalization before signing
46:- **Cedar** (AWS) for policy evaluation

FILE Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/signed-audit-trails/skills/signed-audit-trails-recipe/SKILL.md
1:---
2:name: signed-audit-trails-recipe
3:description: Step-by-step cookbook for setting up cryptographically signed audit trails on Claude Code tool calls. Use when explaining, evaluating, or demonstrating the pattern before committing to the protect-mcp runtime hooks. Covers Cedar policy, Ed25519 receipts, offline verification, tamper detection, CI/CD integration, and SLSA composition.
4:---
5:
6:# Signed Audit Trails for Claude Code Tool Calls
7:
8:Cookbook-style walkthrough for cryptographically signed receipts on every
9:Claude Code tool call. This is the teaching skill. For the runtime
10:implementation, install the [`protect-mcp`](../../protect-mcp/) plugin.
11:
12:## What this gives you
13:
14:Every tool call (`Bash`, `Edit`, `Write`, `WebFetch`) is:
15:
16:1. **Evaluated against a Cedar policy** before execution. If the policy denies
17:   the call, the tool does not run.
18:2. **Signed as an Ed25519 receipt** after execution. Receipts are
19:   JCS-canonical, hash-chained, and verifiable offline by anyone with the
20:   public key.
21:
22:An auditor, regulator, or counterparty can verify the full chain later with a
23:single CLI command (`npx @veritasacta/verify receipts/*.json`). No network
24:call, no vendor lookup, no trust in the operator.
25:
26:## When to use the pattern
27:
28:- **Regulated environments** (finance, healthcare, critical infrastructure)
29:  where you need tamper-evident evidence of agent behavior
30:- **CI/CD pipelines** where you want to prove that a policy gate held for
31:  every automated build step
32:- **Multi-party collaboration** where a counterparty wants to verify your
33:  agent's behavior without trusting your operator
34:- **Compliance contexts** (EU AI Act Article 12, SLSA provenance for
35:  agent-built software) where standard logging is not sufficient
36:
37:## Step 1: Install the hook configuration
38:
39:Create `.claude/settings.json` in your project root:
40:
41:```json
42:{
43:  "hooks": {
44:    "PreToolUse": [
45:      {
46:        "matcher": ".*",
47:        "hook": {
48:          "type": "command",
49:          "command": "npx protect-mcp@latest evaluate --policy ./protect.cedar --tool \"$TOOL_NAME\" --input \"$TOOL_INPUT\" --fail-on-missing-policy false"
50:        }
51:      }
52:    ],
53:    "PostToolUse": [
54:      {
55:        "matcher": ".*",
56:        "hook": {
57:          "type": "command",
58:          "command": "npx protect-mcp@latest sign --tool \"$TOOL_NAME\" --input \"$TOOL_INPUT\" --output \"$TOOL_OUTPUT\" --receipts ./receipts/ --key ./protect-mcp.key"
59:        }
60:      }
61:    ]
62:  }
63:}
64:```
65:
66:The first run of `protect-mcp sign` generates `./protect-mcp.key` (Ed25519
67:private key) if one does not exist. Commit the **public** key fingerprint
68:(visible in any receipt's `public_key` field); do not commit the private
69:key.
70:
71:Add the private key and receipt directory to `.gitignore`:
72:
73:```bash
74:echo "./protect-mcp.key" >> .gitignore
75:echo "./receipts/" >> .gitignore
76:```
77:
78:## Step 2: Write a Cedar policy
79:
80:Create `./protect.cedar`:
81:
82:```cedar
83:// Allow all read-oriented tools by default.
84:permit (
85:    principal,
86:    action in [Action::"Read", Action::"Glob", Action::"Grep", Action::"WebSearch"],
87:    resource
88:);
89:
90:// Allow Bash commands from a safe list only.
91:permit (
92:    principal,
93:    action == Action::"Bash",
94:    resource
95:) when {
96:    context.command_pattern in [
97:        "git", "npm", "pnpm", "yarn", "ls", "cat", "pwd",
98:        "echo", "test", "node", "python", "make"
99:    ]
100:};
101:
102:// Explicit deny on destructive commands. Cedar deny is authoritative.
103:forbid (
104:    principal,
105:    action == Action::"Bash",
106:    resource
107:) when {
108:    context.command_pattern in ["rm -rf", "dd", "mkfs", "shred"]
109:};
110:
111:// Restrict writes to the project directory.
112:permit (
113:    principal,
114:    action in [Action::"Write", Action::"Edit"],
115:    resource
116:) when {
117:    context.path_starts_with == "./"
118:};
119:```
120:
121:Four rules:
122:
123:- Read-oriented tools always allowed
124:- `Bash` allowed for safe command patterns (`git`, `npm`, etc.)
125:- `Bash rm -rf` and similar destructive commands explicitly denied
126:- Writes allowed only within the project (`./` prefix)
127:
128:Cedar `forbid` rules take precedence over `permit` rules, so destructive
129:commands cannot be bypassed by a later permissive rule.
130:
131:## Step 3: Use Claude Code normally
132:
133:Start Claude Code. Every tool call goes through both hooks:
134:
135:```
136:You: Please read the README and summarize it.
137:
138:Claude: I will read README.md.
139:  [PreToolUse: Read ./README.md -> allow]
140:  [Tool: Read executes]
141:  [PostToolUse: receipt rcpt-a8f3c9d2 signed to ./receipts/]
142:
143:... summary of README ...
144:```
145:
146:A session of 20 tool calls produces 20 receipts, each hash-chained to its
147:predecessor.
148:
149:## Step 4: Inspect a receipt
150:
151:```bash
152:cat ./receipts/$(ls -t ./receipts/ | head -1)
153:```
154:
155:```json
156:{
157:  "receipt_id": "rcpt-a8f3c9d2",
158:  "receipt_version": "1.0",
159:  "issuer_id": "claude-code-protect-mcp",
160:  "event_time": "2026-04-17T12:34:56.123Z",
161:  "tool_name": "Read",
162:  "input_hash": "sha256:a3f8c9d2e1b7465f...",
163:  "decision": "allow",
164:  "policy_id": "protect.cedar",
165:  "policy_digest": "sha256:b7e2f4a6c8d0e1f3...",
166:  "parent_receipt_id": "rcpt-3d1ab7c2",
167:  "public_key": "4437ca56815c0516...",
168:  "signature": "4cde814b7889e987..."
169:}
170:```
171:
172:Every field except `signature` and `public_key` is covered by the Ed25519
173:signature. Modifying any field after signing invalidates the signature.
174:
175:## Step 5: Verify the receipt chain
176:
177:```bash
178:npx @veritasacta/verify ./receipts/*.json
179:```
180:
181:Exit codes:
182:
183:| Code | Meaning |
184:|------|---------|
185:| `0`  | All receipts verified; chain intact |
186:| `1`  | A receipt failed signature verification (tampered, or wrong key) |
187:| `2`  | A receipt was malformed |
188:
189:## Step 6: Demonstrate tamper detection
190:
191:Modify any receipt's `decision` field from `allow` to `deny`:
192:
193:```bash
194:python3 -c "
195:import json, os
196:path = './receipts/' + sorted(os.listdir('./receipts'))[-1]
197:r = json.loads(open(path).read())
198:r['decision'] = 'deny'
199:open(path, 'w').write(json.dumps(r))
200:"
201:
202:npx @veritasacta/verify ./receipts/*.json
203:```
204:
205:The verifier exits with code `1` and reports which receipt failed. The
206:Ed25519 signature no longer matches the JCS-canonical bytes of the
207:tampered payload.
208:
209:Restore the field and verification passes again.
210:
211:## How the cryptography works
212:
213:Three invariants make receipts verifiable offline across any conformant
214:implementation:
215:
216:1. **JCS canonicalization (RFC 8785)** before signing. Keys sorted,
217:   whitespace minimized, strings NFC-normalized. Two independent
218:   implementations produce byte-identical signing payloads for the same
219:   receipt content.
220:2. **Ed25519 signatures (RFC 8032)** over the canonical bytes.
221:   Deterministic, fixed-size, no nonce dependency.
222:3. **Hash chain linkage.** Each receipt's `parent_receipt_hash` is the
223:   SHA-256 of the predecessor's canonical form. Insertions, deletions, and
224:   reorderings break later receipts.
225:
226:For the formal wire format see
227:[draft-farley-acta-signed-receipts](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-farley-acta-signed-receipts/).
228:
229:## Cross-implementation interop
230:

20:            "command": "npx protect-mcp@0.5.5 sign --tool \"$TOOL_NAME\" --input \"$TOOL_INPUT\" --output \"$TOOL_OUTPUT\" --receipts \"${REVIEW_GOVERNANCE_RECEIPTS:-./review-receipts/}\" --key \"${REVIEW_GOVERNANCE_KEY:-./review-governance.key}\""
21:          }
22:        ]
23:      }
24:    ]
25:  }

FILE Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance/policies/review-agent-governance.cedar
1:// review-agent-governance: default Cedar policy
2://
3:// Forbids an AI agent from taking consequential review actions without an
4:// explicit human approval signal. A human opens a short approval window by
5:// creating a ./.review-approved flag file (or using the /approve-review
6:// slash command shipped with this plugin). When the flag is present, the
7:// PreToolUse hook short-circuits to permit; when it is absent, this policy
8:// evaluates.
9://
10:// Every tool call, approved or not, still produces an Ed25519 receipt via
11:// the PostToolUse hook. The chain records what was approved and when.
12:
13:// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
14:// Forbid rules: the set of actions that require human approval
15:// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
16:
17:// 1. Posting review-surface GitHub actions via gh CLI
18:forbid (
19:    principal,
20:    action == Action::"Bash",
21:    resource
22:) when {
23:    context.command_pattern in [
24:        "gh pr review",
25:        "gh pr comment",
26:        "gh pr close",
27:        "gh pr merge",
28:        "gh pr edit",
29:        "gh issue comment",
30:        "gh issue close",
31:        "gh issue edit",
32:        "gh release create",
33:        "gh release edit",
34:        "gh api repos"
35:    ]
36:};
37:
38:// 2. Posting via GitLab / Bitbucket CLIs
39:forbid (
40:    principal,
41:    action == Action::"Bash",
42:    resource
43:) when {
44:    context.command_pattern in [
45:        "glab mr comment",
46:        "glab mr approve",
47:        "glab mr merge",
48:        "glab issue comment"
49:    ]
50:};
51:
52:// 3. Git operations on protected branches
53:forbid (
54:    principal,
55:    action == Action::"Bash",
56:    resource
57:) when {
58:    context.command_pattern in ["git push", "git push --force", "git push -f"] &&
59:    context.target_branch in ["main", "master", "release", "production"]
60:};
61:
62:// 4. Direct writes to CI / CD configuration paths. Modifying these files lets
63://    an agent change how future builds or reviews run, bypassing every other
64://    safeguard. Always require human approval.
65:forbid (
66:    principal,
67:    action in [Action::"Write", Action::"Edit"],
68:    resource
69:) when {
70:    context.path_starts_with in [
71:        ".github/workflows/",
72:        ".github/CODEOWNERS",
73:        ".gitlab-ci.yml",
74:        ".circleci/config.yml",
75:        "buildkite/pipeline.yml"
76:    ]
77:};
78:
79:// 5. WebFetch POSTs to known review / chat platforms
80:forbid (
81:    principal,
82:    action == Action::"WebFetch",
83:    resource
84:) when {
85:    context.method == "POST" &&
86:    context.url_host in [
87:        "api.github.com",
88:        "api.gitlab.com",
89:        "api.bitbucket.org",
90:        "hooks.slack.com",
91:        "discord.com"
92:    ]
93:};
94:
95:// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
96:// Permit everything else
97://
98:// This plugin is focused. Non-review actions pass through this policy
99:// unchanged. Run it alongside protect-mcp if you want general tool-call
100:// policy enforcement too.
101:// ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
102:
103:permit (principal, action, resource);
FILE Z:/repos/deps/wshobson-agents/plugins/review-agent-governance/README.md
1:# review-agent-governance
2:
3:Require a human approval signal before an AI agent can post PR reviews,
4:comments, merges, or writes to CI configuration. Built on
5:[`protect-mcp`](https://www.npmjs.com/package/protect-mcp) + Cedar, with
6:every decision producing an Ed25519-signed receipt that verifies offline.
7:
8:## The failure mode this addresses
9:
10:AI agents that post to review surfaces (PR comments, approvals, merges,
11:CI workflow edits) can take actions that affect other contributors,
12:regulated systems, and the integrity of the codebase itself. When the
13:agent hallucinates, mis-reads context, or is tricked into acting
14:incorrectly, the damage is immediate and visible: bogus reviews show up
15:under a real account, merges happen that should not, workflow files get
16:rewritten.
17:
18:This is not a hypothetical. Review bots have posted mass hallucinated
19:review comments, approved PRs they should not have approved, and edited
20:workflow files in ways that compromised other security controls. The
21:pattern is common enough to name: an automated agent is given scope to
22:act on review surfaces, and the lack of a human gate at the moment of
23:action is what turns a localized bug into a public incident.
24:
25:## What the plugin does
26:
27:Two hooks run around every Claude Code tool call:
28:
29:1. **`PreToolUse`** checks for a human approval flag. If absent, evaluates
30:   a Cedar policy (`./review-governance.cedar`) that forbids review-surface
31:   actions unconditionally. Cedar deny means the tool call exits with code
32:   2 and Claude Code blocks it.
33:
34:2. **`PostToolUse`** signs an Ed25519 receipt of the attempt, whether it
35:   was approved, denied, or skipped. The receipt chain records exactly
36:   which actions were authorized and when.
37:
38:Approved windows are opened by creating a `./.review-approved` flag file,
39:or by running the `/approve-review` slash command shipped with this plugin.
40:The window stays open until the flag is removed.
41:
42:## What gets gated
43:
44:The default policy forbids (unless approved):
45:
46:- **`gh pr review`, `gh pr comment`, `gh pr merge`, `gh pr close`, `gh pr edit`**
47:- **`gh issue comment`, `gh issue close`, `gh issue edit`**
48:- **`gh release create`, `gh release edit`**
49:- **`gh api repos`** (catches arbitrary GitHub REST calls)
50:- **GitLab / Bitbucket equivalents** (`glab mr comment` etc.)
51:- **`git push` to `main`, `master`, `release`, `production`**
52:- **Writes to `.github/workflows/`, `.gitlab-ci.yml`, `.circleci/config.yml`**
53:- **`WebFetch` POSTs to `api.github.com`, `hooks.slack.com`, Discord**
54:
55:Everything else passes through. This plugin is focused on the review
56:surface; use it alongside [protect-mcp](../protect-mcp/) if you want
57:general tool-call policy enforcement.
58:
59:## Installation
60:
61:```bash
62:claude plugin install wshobson/agents/review-agent-governance
63:```
64:
65:Copy the default policy into your project:
66:
67:```bash
68:cp .claude/plugins/review-agent-governance/policies/review-agent-governance.cedar \
69:   ./review-governance.cedar
70:```
71:
72:Then either:
73:
74:- **(Recommended)** keep hooks active for every session and open approval
75:  windows explicitly before review actions, or
76:- Set `REVIEW_APPROVAL_FLAG=./never-approve` to effectively disable the
77:  approval bypass (forces every review action through Cedar).
78:
79:## Opening an approval window
80:
81:### Flag file
82:
83:```bash
84:touch ./.review-approved
85:# Let the agent perform the approved action
86:rm ./.review-approved
87:```
88:
89:### Slash command (from inside Claude Code)
90:
91:```
92:/approve-review "Posting the code review for #123"
93:```
94:
95:The command creates `./.review-approved` with a note describing the
96:approval reason and appends a JSON entry under
97:`./review-receipts/approvals/`.
98:
99:**Important note on the approval log:** entries under
100:`./review-receipts/approvals/*.json` are **plain JSON records, not signed
101:receipts**. They do not flow through `protect-mcp sign`, so
102:`@veritasacta/verify` does not cover them. The approval log is
103:operator-trust; it records what the human intended to approve but can be
104:edited after the fact without detection.
105:
106:What IS signed and tamper-evident: the `PostToolUse` tool-call receipts
107:that every action (allowed or denied) produces under
108:`./review-receipts/*.json`. Those are the authoritative audit trail. Use
109:`npx @veritasacta/verify ./review-receipts/*.json` to verify them.
110:
111:If you need signed approval records as well (for regulated environments),
112:run them through protect-mcp directly, or emit them as separate receipts
113:via `npx protect-mcp@latest sign --tool approve-review --input ...`.
114:
115:### Listing pending or denied actions
116:
117:```
118:/list-pending
119:```
120:
121:Walks the receipt chain at `./review-receipts/` and prints any recent
122:`decision: deny` entries, so you can see what the agent tried to do that
123:was blocked.
124:
125:### A note on what the signed chain covers
126:
127:When the approval flag is present, the `PreToolUse` hook short-circuits
128:to `exit 0` without calling `protect-mcp evaluate`. The downstream
129:`PostToolUse` receipt for that approved action will therefore have
130:`decision: allow` but no `policy_digest` field, because no Cedar policy
131:was evaluated. Auditors walking the chain should expect this: an approved
132:tool call shows up as a signed receipt with `reason: human_approved` and
133:no policy reference. Denied tool calls and non-review actions (which do
134:go through Cedar) carry the `policy_digest` as usual.
135:
136:## Example session
137:
138:An agent working on a PR wants to post a review comment. Without approval:
139:
140:```
141:$ agent: gh pr review 42 --comment --body "LGTM"
142:  → PreToolUse hook runs
143:  → No ./.review-approved file, policy evaluates
144:  → Cedar: forbid on context.command_pattern == "gh pr review"
145:  → Exit 2: Claude Code blocks the tool call
146:  → PostToolUse runs, signs a receipt with decision=deny
147:```
148:
149:With approval:
150:
151:```
152:$ touch ./.review-approved
153:$ agent: gh pr review 42 --comment --body "LGTM"
154:  → PreToolUse hook runs
155:  → ./.review-approved present, exit 0
156:  → Tool call proceeds
157:  → PostToolUse signs a receipt (decision=allow, reason=human_approved)
158:$ rm ./.review-approved
159:```
160:
161:The receipt chain at `./review-receipts/` records both attempts: the
162:initial deny and the subsequent allow after approval. An auditor reading
163:the chain later can see exactly which actions were human-gated and when.
164:
165:## Composing with protect-mcp
166:
167:This plugin focuses on review-surface actions specifically. For general
168:policy enforcement across all Claude Code tool calls, install
169:[protect-mcp](../protect-mcp/) alongside it. They compose naturally:
170:
171:- `protect-mcp` evaluates a general policy (e.g., deny `rm -rf`, restrict
172:  `Write` to project root) for every tool call
173:- `review-agent-governance` adds the review-surface gate on top
174:
175:Both hooks run, both produce receipts. Configure different receipt
176:directories (`./receipts/` and `./review-receipts/`) to keep the chains
177:separate if that helps your audit workflow.
178:
179:## Why Cedar, why receipts
180:
181:**Cedar** (AWS's open authorization engine) expresses policy declaratively
182:and formally. Reviewers read the policy to understand exactly what is
183:gated without reading code. Policies type-check with `cedar validate`.
184:Changes to the policy are diffable.
185:
186:**Ed25519 receipts** (RFC 8032, JCS canonicalization per RFC 8785,
187:hash-chained) provide tamper-evident evidence that does not depend on the
188:operator. Any party with the public key can run
189:`npx @veritasacta/verify ./review-receipts/*.json` and get an exit code
190:that proves every receipt is authentic and the chain is intact. If any
191:receipt was altered after signing, verification fails with exit 1.
192:
193:## Standards
194:
195:- **Ed25519** (RFC 8032) for receipt signatures
196:- **JCS** (RFC 8785) for deterministic canonicalization before signing
197:- **Cedar** (AWS) for declarative, formally verifiable policy evaluation
198:- **IETF draft** [draft-farley-acta-signed-receipts](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-farley-acta-signed-receipts/) for receipt format
199:
200:## Related
201:
202:- [`protect-mcp`](../protect-mcp/) — general Cedar + receipt enforcement
203:  for all Claude Code tool calls
204:- [`protect-mcp` on npm](https://www.npmjs.com/package/protect-mcp) — the
205:  runtime this plugin depends on
206:- [`@veritasacta/verify`](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@veritasacta/verify)
207:  — offline receipt verification CLI
208:- [Cedar for AI agents](https://github.com/cedar-policy/cedar-for-agents)

.claude/rules\research-protocol.md:12:description: Cardinal-rule body for research-first discipline — RECALL → INVESTIGATE → VERIFY before any change; tool routing table; RPI 3-phase workflow; benchmark-first sub-rule; repo-discovery sub-rule; multi-catalog discovery surface. Cite ALWAYS.
.claude/rules\research-protocol.md:40:When the question is "what's the SOTA for X?" or "which repo should we adopt for X?", start from leaderboards/benchmarks BEFORE topic-keyword search. The traditional flow (`github search topic` → top stars → audit) biases toward popular-but-not-measured-best repos. Instead:

docs\v64-stable-prefix-convention.md:79:**Hash-domain canonicalization** (per Wave 84 codex T1 F-2 P2 fix-forward): `stable_prefix_hash` covers ONLY the in-message-stream prefix content (system + developer + initial_user messages + any in-stream project_context up to `prefix_frozen_at_message_index`). Provider-native tool/schema/cache-control blobs are SEPARATELY represented by their sibling hashes/policy-IDs to prevent double-counting. If a runtime injects tool/schema text directly into a message body (rare but possible), that text contributes to `stable_prefix_hash` AND those tool/schema definitions ALSO contribute to their sibling hashes — operator must avoid this double-injection per AAP L141 "never modify existing messages" discipline.
docs\v64-stable-prefix-convention.md:81:**`cache_control_policy_id` provider-translation note** (per Wave 84 codex T1 F-4 P3 fix-forward): `cache_control_policy_id` is an eee-local policy identifier, NOT necessarily a provider-literal API enum. Anthropic API today exposes `cache_control: {type: "ephemeral"}`; "persistent" was removed from the enum above because no provider currently honors a persistent cache scope. Provider request translation (mapping policy-ID → API parameter) remains owned by the routing/runtime layer (Wave 83 §1 + future tools/v64_cache_locality_verify.py).

The revolution is here. I've worked with thousands of startups \u2014 Coinbase, Instacart, Rippling \u2014 when they were one or two people in a garage. Before YC, I was one of the first eng/PM/designers at Palantir, cofounded Posterous (sold to Twitter), and built Bookface, YC's internal social network.\n\n**gstack is my answer.** I've been building products for twenty years, and right now I'm shipping more products than I ever have. In the last 60 days: 3 production services, 40+ shipped features, part-time, while running YC full-time. On logical code change \u2014 not raw LOC, which AI inflates \u2014 my 2026 run rate is **~810\u00d7 my 2013 pace** (11,417 vs 14 logical lines/day). Year-to-date (through April 18), 2026 has already produced **240\u00d7 the entire 2013 year**. Measured across 40 public + private `garrytan/*` repos including Bookface, after excluding one demo repo. AI wrote most of it. The point isn't who typed it, it's what shipped.\n\n> The LOC critics aren't wrong that raw line counts inflate with AI. They are wrong that normalized-for-inflation, I'm less productive. I'm more productive, by a lot. Different era. The difference is the tooling.\n\n**gstack is how I do it.** It turns Claude Code into a virtual engineering team \u2014 a CEO who rethinks the product, an eng manager who locks architecture, a designer who catches AI slop, a reviewer who finds production bugs, a QA lead who opens a real browser, a security officer who runs OWASP + STRIDE audits, and a release engineer who ships the PR. Twenty-three specialists and eight power tools, all slash commands, all Markdown, all free, MIT license.\n\nThis is my open source software factory. I use it every day. docs\sota-architecture-audit\future-evolution\_extension-and-reaudit-metadata.json:475:    "readme_head_2000": "# Superpowers\n\nSuperpowers is a complete software development methodology for your coding agents, built on top of a set of composable skills and some initial instructions that make sure your agent uses them.\n\n## Quickstart\n\nGive your agent Superpowers: [Claude Code](#claude-code), [Codex CLI](#codex-cli), [Codex App](#codex-app), [Factory Droid](#factory-droid), [Gemini CLI](#gemini-cli), [OpenCode](#opencode), [Cursor](#cursor), [GitHub Copilot CLI](#github-copilot-cli).\n\n## How it works\n\nIt starts from the moment you fire up your coding agent. As soon as it sees that you're building something, it *doesn't* just jump into trying to write code. Instead, it steps back and asks you what you're really trying to do. \n\nOnce it's teased a spec out of the conversation, it shows it to you in chunks short enough to actually read and digest. \n\nAfter you've signed off on the design, your agent puts together an implementation plan that's clear enough for an enthusiastic junior engineer with poor taste, no judgement, no project context, and an aversion to testing to follow. It emphasizes true red/green TDD, YAGNI (You Aren't Gonna Need It), and DRY. \n\nNext up, once you say \"go\", it launches a *subagent-driven-development* process, having agents work through each engineering task, inspecting and reviewing their work, and continuing forward. It's not uncommon for Claude to be able to work autonomously for a couple hours at a time without deviating from the plan you put together.\n\nThere's a bunch more to it, but that's the core of the system. And because the skills trigger automatically, you don't need to do anything special. Your coding agent just has Superpowers.\n\n\n## Sponsorship\n\nIf Superpowers has helped you do stuff that makes money and you are so inclined, I'd greatly appreciate it if you'd consider [sponsoring my opensource work](https://github.com/sponsors/obra).\n\nThanks! \n\n- Jesse\n\n\n## Installation\n\nInstallation differs by harness. If you use more than one, install Superpo"
